- **#452** unified the catalog tabs and the search scopes into `CatalogScopeView`, and three more tests went with it — the record, the search field's Cancel, and the swipe row's close-on-tap.
- **#470** moved routine detail off `pushedScreenChrome` to a system large title for the native collapse. The system supplies Back, so the custom `backButton` key went with the chrome, and every test that popped that screen by identifier stopped finding anything.

**The fix is a suite that survives the next chrome change, not one patched to today's identifiers.** Popping a pushed screen goes through `tapBack`, which tries the custom key, falls back to the navigation bar's leading item, and fails with an inventory of what WAS on screen. That inventory is the second half: **a remote session cannot reach the `ui-screenshots` artifact** (blob storage, blocked at CONNECT) but the `::error::` annotations carry 400 characters of assertion text through the API — so a failed query that prints what it saw is the only diagnostic channel a sandbox actually has. Three fragile assertions now carry one.

**And job logs were reachable the whole time.** `mcp__github__get_job_logs` with `return_content: true` serves them; only artifacts are genuinely blocked. The ci-status skill had claimed otherwise since 2026-07-08 and sent sessions hunting for evidence they could already read. Corrected there.

**The process half: a red `ui-test` on main now files ONE tracking issue and then stays quiet until it is closed.** Making it required was considered and rejected — it is the slowest job and has two documented flake modes, so a degraded runner would block every merge, which is a worse failure than the one being fixed. Filing once turns a silent red into a backlog item exactly once; a later red push adds nothing, so the signal cannot become noise that gets muted. What it does NOT do is claim the check is trustworthy enough to gate a release: the standing advice stays "check `ui-test` on main before cutting a build", now written into the ci-status skill.

**2026-07-28 — A form that validates against a `@Query` must exempt the row it just wrote** — Dave: saving a brand-new custom exercise flashed "An exercise with this name already exists." on the way out. It did, and the editor was right to: `save()` inserts the `Exercise` into the context, the `@Query allExercises` sees it on the very next pass, and the sheet keeps rendering through its dismissal animation. On the create path the duplicate check excluded `editingExercise?.name`, which is nil — so for those last frames the field compared the draft to the row it had just created, went red, and dimmed Save under the user's finger as the sheet slid away.

**The rename path had the same bug and no one had reported it yet**: there the exclusion is the OLD name, and `apply(to:)` has already written the NEW one, so the freshly renamed exercise collides with itself the same way. One `savedName` state set at the top of `save()` covers both — `excludedName` is `savedName ?? editingExercise?.name`, feeding the warning and `canSave` alike. Validation stays LIVE through the dismissal rather than being switched off by an `isSaving` flag; the rule is simply that an exercise is never its own duplicate, whether it is the one being edited or the one this editor just saved.

The general shape, now a rule in `.claude/rules/swiftdata.md`: **an editor's write is visible to its own `@Query` before the editor is gone.** Anything the form derives from that query — collision warnings, enable/disable state, counts — has to exempt what the form itself just wrote, or it renders a frame of self-contradiction on the way out. Dismissal is not instant, and the frames after the write are still the user's.

**2026-07-28 (last) — The hand-rolled in-sheet stage slide is retired: Today's last two trays push for real** — the follow-up #463 named and deliberately left out. `SwapInSheet` (start a workout → choose a routine) and `ScheduleRoutineTray` (pick a routine → schedule it) were the last two genuine drill-ins still animating a `ZStack` with `.move` transitions; both are `NavigationStack`s now, which is what the app already did two surfaces over. Nothing about them was tunable: the failure list is in .claude/rules/ui-interaction.md and every item is a thing the system does for free.

**The host owns the stack, and that turned out to be the interesting part.** `ScheduleEditor` carried its OWN `NavigationStack` — added when its schedule-mode row needed to push and neither embed context supplied one. Converting `ScheduleRoutineTray` would have nested a stack inside a stack, so the stack moved UP: `ScheduleTray` and `ScheduleRoutineTray` each supply one and hide its root bar, and the editor went back to being plain content. General rule now: the view that owns the HEADER owns the stack; an embedded view that brings navigation chrome will collide with the first host that has its own.

**`SwapInSheet` binds a path instead of a `NavigationLink`, because its DETENT rides the depth.** Its root is two keys tall (a compact `@ScaledMetric` detent) and its pushed picker is a routine list, so the sheet has to grow. The stage version set the detent in the same function that set the stage; a bare `NavigationLink` would have left nothing to observe. `NavigationStack(path:)` + `.onChange(of: path.count)` covers every way the depth can change — ⚠️ including the back SWIPE, which is exactly the interaction a button-set flag can never see, and exactly the one the conversion was for.

Two things fell out for free: `ScheduleRoutineTray`'s `.id(picked.persistentModelID)` re-seed hack is gone (a closure destination builds a fresh `ScheduleEditor` per push, so its `@State` seeds itself), and both hand-rolled 32 pt chevron back keys are gone with their `trayBackButton` identifiers — nothing referenced them.

`GitHubSyncTray` deliberately keeps the slide: three steps with Continue/Back is a stepper, not navigation. There is nothing to go "back" to in the navigation sense, and its steps are the same height.

⚠️ Device pass, and it is the whole point of the change: the back swipe out of both pushed screens, the sheet height settling on `SwapInSheet`'s push and pop (the detent follows `path.count`, so a swipe that is abandoned halfway should leave it where it was), and the pushed titles reading right at large Dynamic Type.

**2026-07-28 (later still) — The editor's two multi-selects are ONE interaction: picked chips + a pushed searchable list; and drilling in inside a sheet is a `NavigationStack`, never a hand-rolled stage slide** — Dave, on the first cut: "I feel like 11 chips is overwhelming." They were, but the eleven chips were the smaller half of the problem. The section directly beneath them — REQUIRES, the equipment multi-select — was a SwiftUI `Menu` over the whole catalog. Two adjacent multi-selects in one sheet behaving differently is the actual smell, and the one with a hundred options had the worse control.

**A `Menu` is the wrong shape for a multi-select, and it gets worse the longer the list is.** It can't be searched; it closes on every pick, so adding four pieces of equipment meant opening it four times; and it shows no selection state, so it could only ever ADD, which is why removal had to live somewhere else entirely (tapping the chip). Both sections are now the same thing: the chips show what you PICKED, and one `+ Add` key pushes a list where selection toggles in place and you leave when you're done. Equipment's list is flat, alphabetical and searched (the presented-catalog law, 2026-07-25); muscle groups' is eleven rows grouped by region with no field, since a list you can see all of has nothing to search and an empty field over eleven rows is a keyboard waiting to cover them. `MuscleGroup.grouped` had been sitting in Kit unused since it was written, waiting for a surface that wanted structure.

⚠️ **Drilling in inside a sheet is a `NavigationStack` with the root nav bar hidden — the pattern `ScheduleTray` and the Voice-cues row already use — and NOT the ZStack + `.move` stage slide** (`SwapInSheet`, `ScheduleRoutineTray`). Dave: "The schedule routine tray's in-sheet nav seems kinda janky." It is, and the reasons are structural rather than tunable: there is **no interactive back-swipe** (the only exit is a 32 pt chevron); **both stages exist mid-transition** at different intrinsic heights, so the sheet resolves to the taller one and settles; **the header sits outside the ZStack**, so the title hard-swaps while the content slides, two motions for one gesture; `.clipped()` fights the sheet's own corner radius; and VoiceOver gets a bare chevron instead of a system Back carrying the origin's title. A `NavigationStack` gives all of that away for free, plus Reduce Motion and RTL, and the app was already doing it correctly two surfaces over. `SheetPickList` is the multi-select sibling of `NavigationSelectRow`, and carries the law in its doc comment.

**Follow-up, deliberately not in this PR:** `ScheduleRoutineTray` and `SwapInSheet` are both genuine drill-ins still hand-rolling the slide and should move to `NavigationStack` — separate surfaces, separate unit of work. `GitHubSyncTray` is NOT the same shape: a linear three-step wizard with Continue/Back is a stepper, not navigation, and the slide is defensible there.

Same round, the chip grammar: the muscle chip is the equipment chip's twin (r11, tap to remove), and the presentation-level modifiers moved OUTSIDE the `NavigationStack` so they address the sheet rather than its root screen. ⚠️ **The last remaining group's chip is DISABLED and drops its ✕** rather than swallowing the tap — a control that looks live and does nothing is the dead-tap class (build 76). Its list row is disabled the same way, with the footer carrying the rule the row can't state. The form caption names what the chips are ("Every group this works. Chest leads."); the list's footer carries the floor ("Keep at least one.").

⚠️ Device pass, on top of the previous entry's: the push and the back-swipe out of both lists, the search field over the equipment catalog with the keyboard up on a sheet, and the disabled last chip reading as deliberate rather than broken.

**2026-07-28 — An exercise works SEVERAL muscle groups, and the editor multi-selects them** — Dave: "Shouldn't muscle group be a multi-select in Edit exercise?" It should. `Exercise.muscleGroup` was a single enum, so the model forced a lie on almost every compound: Bench Press was chest and its triceps existed nowhere, Deadlift was back and its hamstrings existed nowhere. Typing "triceps" couldn't find a bench press, a dip couldn't be offered as its substitute, and the editor's chips were radio buttons over a fact that isn't one.

**The list is ORDERED and the first entry is the primary**, which is what keeps this additive rather than a migration. `muscleGroup` stays exactly what it was — the group the move is FOR, what every single-group reader sees, the interchange's required field — and `muscleGroups[0]` always equals it. That is the same shape `metricProfile` has beside `exerciseType`, and the setter enforces it the same way (writing the list re-stamps the column, so the two can never disagree). In the editor the primary is simply the first chip you tap: no ranking UI, no second concept, and deselecting it promotes the next. The last chip can't be deselected, and the caption says so rather than letting a tap do nothing.

**Storage is an explicit OVERRIDE, not a copy — nil means "follow the catalog."** `muscleGroupsData` is an additive optional `Data?` (lightweight migration, existing rows read nil), and resolution mirrors `metricProfile`: explicit list → the built-in's catalog row → `[muscleGroup]`. So the catalog pass below reaches every existing store with zero writes, and a user who edits one exercise doesn't freeze the other 250 against future authoring. ⚠️ **A one-element list is meaningful storage, not noise.** The first cut dropped single-group lists on encode (mirroring the DTO's byte-cleanliness rule) — which meant pruning a built-in's secondaries stored nothing, fell straight back through to the catalog, and handed them back on the next read. Encode any non-empty list; let the DTO decide what to write.

**The interchange writes `muscleGroups` only when an explicit list exists**, so pre-field files and untouched exercises stay byte-identical, and a restored store keeps picking up catalog authoring. ⚠️ It also had to join the **built-in export filter** (`isFavorite || notes || video || hasDefaultTargets || metricsData`), or a built-in customized ONLY by its muscle groups would never be written at all — the customization would survive in the store and vanish on backup. The validator rejects a list that leads with anything but `muscleGroup`, repeats a group, or is empty: those are hand-edit failures that would silently change which muscle an exercise reads as, depending on which field a reader trusts.

**The catalog was authored, all 163 rows that earned it** (Dave chose the full pass over shipping the capability alone: nearly every exercise anyone opens is a built-in, so a multi-select over a single-group catalog is an empty room). The rule, which lives on the `e` builder: the primary never moves; a secondary earns its place only if someone would program the move to train it; isolation stays isolated (a Biceps Curl is biceps, full stop); `.fullBody` takes secondaries only where a group genuinely dominates; stretches and mobility drills name what they lengthen and keep one group. **Core is deliberately absent as a secondary almost everywhere** — it braces in nearly everything, so listing it would say nothing.

**What the groups actually feed, and what they deliberately do not.** Search reaches every group, so "triceps" finds Bench Press — the biggest win, and the reason the Exercises surface still needs no Muscle facet. Operator's muscle filter became `contains`, because "chest exercises" means every move that works the chest. The similarity ranker went **primary-weighted, not flat Jaccard**: overlap alone would score a Dumbbell Fly (chest) at 1/3 against a Bench Press and a Skull Crusher (triceps · chest) at 2/3, ranking the best chest swap in the catalog below a triceps move — breadth is not distance. 70% of the muscle score rides the primary, 30% the overlap. `swapSuggestions` widened its pool to shares-ANY-group (a dip is a real bench substitute and files under triceps), which is safe precisely because it is ranked. Three things stayed on the primary on purpose: **`kitDoableAlternatives`** (sorted by name, no ranking to protect quality, so widening it would seat a pushdown among the offered replacements for a bench press), **`Routine.focusLabel` and the template's muscle line** (folding in secondaries makes everything full body — a bench-and-row session would claim five groups, which is true and says nothing), and **the catalog ROW capsule**. ⚠️ That last one is the flag-don't-hide law (#113) doing its job: the row leads with muscle capsules and ends with gear, amber-first, so that overflow can only ever drop an AVAILABLE piece. Three muscle capsules is exactly what would push the amber "needs X" into "N more". A row is an identity line; the full set reads on the detail screen, the planning sheet and the config sheet, which wrap instead of overflowing.

Kit 515 + CLI 26 green on Linux. ⚠️ Device pass: the chips at large Dynamic Type (eleven of them now toggle rather than switch), the wrapped capsule rows on both sheets, and whether the row showing only the primary reads as a gap once the detail shows three.
**2026-07-28 (last of the day, ii) — The agent-doc line-length cap covers `.claude/rules/` too, and `app-surfaces.md`'s "surface by surface" section is a description, not a changelog** — a survey of every `.md` in the repo after the CLAUDE.md restructure, asking which others had the same disease.

**Almost none did, and that is the finding.** The `1 insertion, 1 deletion` rewrite pattern was unique to CLAUDE.md. `PLATFORM.md` is 30 KB but its longest line is 542 chars; the skills and agent files are all small and hard-wrapped; `docs/HANDOFF-2026-07-07.md` is self-labelled a historical snapshot "kept as-committed for provenance", is referenced by nothing and auto-loads into nothing — an honest archive, deliberately LEFT ALONE. ⚠️ **`docs/DECISIONS.md` stays exempt from every budget**: it is append-only (`28/0`, `40/0`, `22/0` — zero deletions ever), its longest line is 10.8 KB of one historical entry rather than a growing tail, and it holds the *why* that git cannot reconstruct.

**The one real offender was `.claude/rules/app-surfaces.md`, and its size was the least of it.** Its "What the app is, surface by surface" section was a single 8.7 KB line — a chronological accretion (design v3 → v4 → the build-27 and build-28/29 rounds → the 2026-07-17 equipment rebuild) grown by appending a dated paragraph per round. ⚠️ **It had drifted into being WRONG**: it opened "the app is four bottom tabs … Today · Routines · Exercises · Equipment", when `RootTabView` has carried FIVE since build 138 (Today · Routines · Exercises · **Kit** · Search(role)) and "Equipment"→"Kit" is a standing vocabulary law. It also still described `CatalogBrowseScreen`, `EquipmentCatalogScreen` and the header-magnifier search, all deleted. A rules file auto-loads on every `PlusPlus/**` touch, so this was misinforming every app session — worse than merely long.

**Replaced with a short, current, hard-wrapped description**, carrying forward the four things that lived ONLY there (the Live Activity phases, the `WidgetSnapshot`/App-Group/App-Intents chain, the no-phone-notifications rule from #322, and the crash-orphan salvage on Today's appearance) — each re-verified against code before it was written down, not copied. The removed history is in this log under its dated entries. The section now opens by saying it is a description and not a changelog: **correct a claim when the app changes; don't append a dated paragraph beside it.** 53 KB → 47 KB, longest line 8,710 → 117.

**The CI guard generalized accordingly**: the ~2 KB line cap now applies to CLAUDE.md AND every `.claude/rules/*.md`; the 25 KB byte budget stays CLAUDE.md-only, since rules files are path-scoped and load lazily, so length is fine there and a mega-line is not. ⚠️ The lesson worth keeping is that the cap earns its place twice over — a mega-line is both the thing that makes every concurrent branch conflict AND the shape that lets a paragraph rot unnoticed, because nobody re-reads 8.7 KB to check it is still true.

**2026-07-28 (last of the day) — CLAUDE.md's Current State is a bounded, one-item-per-line section, and its size budget is measured in BYTES, not lines** — Dave: "What the fuck is up with our massive CLAUDE.md? And it seems like we are constantly hitting merge conflicts with it." Both halves turned out to be the same fact.

**The file was 371 KB in 157 lines, and ONE line was 310 KB of that** (a second was 45 KB; together 96% of the file). ⚠️ **That single line was the merge-conflict engine, and the mechanism is the part worth keeping**: git merges line by line, and every session prepended its update to that same line rather than adding one — `git log --numstat` shows `1 insertion, 1 deletion` on every commit touching CLAUDE.md for months. So *any* two concurrent branches conflicted on it, always, by construction, presenting a 310 KB hunk with no internal structure to resolve against. It was never bad luck or a bad merge strategy; it was the shape of the file. **One item per line is what makes concurrent branches mergeable** — that is the whole reason for the new format, not tidiness.

**Line count is the wrong ruler, and that is why nobody caught it.** The old hygiene rule said "keep this file under ~200 lines" — at 157 lines the file *passed*, while the section header directly above the 310 KB line read "Keep this section current and SHORT." A rule that measures lines cannot see a file that grows sideways. The budget is now **25 KB whole file / ~2 KB any single line**, enforced as the FIRST step of the already-required `kit-test` job — riding an existing required check means the budget binds without a branch-protection ruleset change (⚠️ consequence: kit-test can go red for a docs reason before Swift runs; the ci-status skill says so).

**Deleting the section lost nothing, and that was verified rather than assumed.** The line held 210 ⚠️ law markers, so the plan was to relocate them — but an audit of 28 distinctive law phrases found **100% already covered** in `.claude/rules/` or this log. `app-surfaces.md:150-199` carries the `.principal`-is-a-TITLE-VIEW law with its UIKit-centring reasoning; `:325` carries the `.soft` bottom scroll edge effect; the search-scope, `sharedBackgroundVisibility`, `avoidHidingContent`, accessory and morph laws are all there. The line was pure duplication — 707 segments collapsing to 174 unique (75% verbatim repeats), with "Build 120"/"117"/"107" each appearing six times. The tell was **135 occurrences of "See docs/DECISIONS.md \<date\>"**: the file said the canonical account lived here, then restated it inline anyway, 135 times.

**docs/DECISIONS.md was examined and deliberately left ALONE.** The premise that it also needed shrinking did not survive checking: its longest line is 10.8 KB against CLAUDE.md's 310 KB, and its commits are pure append (`28/0`, `40/0`, `22/0` — zero deletions, ever), spread across real multi-line entries, so two branches inserting near the top produce a small resolvable hunk. It is also the one artifact that cannot be reconstructed — git records *what* changed, this file records *why*. A ledger growing is a ledger working; if it becomes hard to use the answer is an index, not deletion. ⚠️ **Do not "shorten" this file** — the byte budget applies to CLAUDE.md only.

**Current State now carries only what is live** (~15 lines, newest first, item deleted once shipped and validated), with detail going to a dated entry here and durable laws to `.claude/rules/` with `paths:` frontmatter. Result: 371 KB → 18 KB, longest line 310,417 → 1,348 chars. App/Kit/CLI code untouched.

**2026-07-28 — The horizontal ticker takes every MEASURED metric; only the two enumerated scales keep a wheel** — Dave, from a screenshot of the exercise editor's DEFAULTS tray: "We have a mix of different types of inputs in the trays that come up for setting these default values. I like our custom horizontal ticker. Seems others could and should use that."

**The line drawn: a measured quantity scrubs; an enumerated scale wheels.** The tape scrubber shipped for time (2026-07-15), then distance and calories (2026-07-19), on the reasoning "wide continuous range, where a uniform wheel would be an unwieldy list". That framing was about the wheel's LENGTH, and it left an arbitrary-looking residue: a 400-row weight wheel and a 300-row power wheel stayed, while a 2000-row calorie tape had already proven the control. The better framing is what the control SAYS. A tape is a ruler — it asserts that the value lives on a continuum and shows its neighbours in both directions — and it reaches every value between its marks, where a wheel can only ever land on its own grid. That describes a load, a rep count, a height, a split, a speed, a grade, a wattage and a cadence. So all nine joined the five already there. What's left on the wheel is **machine resistance** (30 numbered levels) and **RPE** (a subjective 1–10 rating): metrics whose values ARE the list, where a ruler would claim a precision that doesn't exist and a ten-stop ruler is worse than a ten-row wheel anyway. `usesTapeScrubber` carries the rule and stays exhaustive, so a new metric must decide rather than fall to the wheel by default.

**Precision the wheel couldn't reach, per metric.** Weight and assist address HALF A POUND (a quarter kilo), finer than a microplate, so 137.5 lb or a 61.25 kg stack is exact — marks still on the wheel's old 2.5 lb / 1.25 kg grid, labels every 10 lb / 5 kg. Pace addresses the SECOND on the road, where the wheel offered 5 s steps and a 7:58 mile was simply unpickable. Speed and incline address tenths, marked on the half — a treadmill's own dial. Reps, height, power and cadence address whole units across their full range. Every tape is bounded by the metric's own `range`, enforced for all 16 metrics × both unit systems by a new well-formedness test that also checks the label stride sits on the minor grid and that a mid-tape unit round-trips through the geometry. `scrubberTape` gained a `weightUnit:` parameter for the three weight-denominated metrics, and `WorkoutMetric.range(weightUnit:distanceUnit:)` went public to feed it — a tape built from the unit-less `range` would let a kg height scrub past its own ceiling.

**The rep-target tray was the last picker still spinning, and it now scrubs too — with no "off" switch, because `RepTarget` already owns that rule.** It was two side-by-side wheels ("Reps" and "Up to"), the one control in the defaults tray that would still have wheeled while every row under it scrubbed. It is now one big readout over two tapes. ⚠️ The upper bound needs no sentinel and no toggle: `RepTarget` already collapses an upper at or below the target to nil, so **parking the second caret on the first number IS "no range"**, and pushing it right opens one ("10" → "10–14"). That's also why a nil upper seeds at `lower` rather than at a floor — an untouched sheet saves back exactly what it opened with, and the left half of that tape is an honest "no upper bound" rather than a dead zone. `RepTargetWheelSheet` → `RepTargetSheet` at its three call sites.

**Deliberately NOT converted.** The Sets stepper (1–20, ±1) has no tray to convert — it is steppers inline in a card. The heart-rate custom range is two ±5 steppers inside an expanding section of the zone picker, not a tray, and its two values are coupled by a clamp. `MetricWheelSheet` keeps its name even though it now presents a tape for 14 of 16 metrics: renaming it would orphan every reference in this log for a doc-comment's worth of clarity.

**Same round, second pass: every tray's top row is a `SheetHeader`, and the metric trays stop answering in a different button vocabulary than the sheet that opens them** — Dave: "make sure we are consistent with the style of buttons we use in trays across the app, like for the top buttons (close, save, whatever)."

A sweep of all 23 files that present a sheet found the grammar already near-universal: a `SheetHeader` (title upper-left; optional Cancel text key; a `primaryFill` capsule commit) or, for a picker where tapping a row IS the action, its `closeOnly` `SheetDismissKey`. Four surfaces were out.

**`MetricWheelSheet` and the rep tray wore a system navigation bar.** Both were `NavigationStack` + `.navigationTitle` + a `.confirmationAction` toolbar `Button("Done")` — one tinted accent green, one unstyled. That is the whole complaint in one gesture: you open the exercise editor (a `SheetHeader` with Cancel and a filled Save), tap a defaults row, and the tray that opens answers with a system nav bar and a green text button. Both now lead with `SheetHeader(closeOnly:)`. ⚠️ **`closeOnly` is a claim about the commit, not just the chrome** — the label says "leave", so the value has to already be saved. The metric tray always was (the scrubber commits on settle, the wheel on selection). The rep tray was NOT: it held both values in `@State` and fired a single `onSave` from Done, so the identical-looking corner button meant "commit" on one row of the defaults list and "leave" on the next. It now commits on every settle like its neighbour. (Reading `target` inside the settle closure is live, not stale: `@State`'s `wrappedValue` reads its storage box, which is why `MetricScrubberPane` can already read `unit` from `onDisappear`.)

**`IncrementSheet` had no top row at all** — it led with an ALL-CAPS mono `INCREMENT`, which is the SECTION vocabulary, not the title one, and it offered no way out but a swipe, making it the only tray in the app with nothing in its top-right corner. Now `SheetHeader(title: "Increment", closeOnly: true)`. ⚠️ Its container `.padding(20)` became horizontal + bottom only: `SheetHeader` carries its own 24 pt of grabber clearance, and stacking a container top inset on that pushes the title twice as far down as every other tray's.

**`ExerciseInfoView` was deleted** rather than restyled. Nothing has constructed it since `ExerciseDetailSheet` took its job, its doc comment claimed a caller that doesn't exist, and it was the app's last stock-SwiftUI surface — a `List` of `LabeledContent` under a system nav bar with an unstyled toolbar `Done` — so it was carrying a fourth button vocabulary that no reachable screen used.

**And the four hand-rolled tray headers moved to 24 pt of top padding.** `SwapInSheet`, `ScheduleRoutineTray`, `GitHubSyncTray` and `OperatorTray` legitimately build their own header rows (a back chevron, a glyph, a status word — things `SheetHeader` can't express) and all four already used `SheetDismissKey` and the same `.title3` bold title. But all four sat at `.padding(.top, 14)` while `SheetHeader` uses 24 — a value chosen deliberately on 2026-07-24 because 14 was cramped under the sheet's drag grabber at `.medium`, from Dave's own screenshot. The fix landed in `SheetHeader` and never reached its four hand-rolled cousins.

**Left alone deliberately, with reasons.** `SessionOverviewSheet`, `ExerciseConfigSheet` and `HealthStartPrimer` put their commit at the BOTTOM as a full-width key ("Back to now", "Add to workout", the Health ask) — that is a different and coherent composition for a full-height sheet, and bolting a top-right Done onto them would give each two dismissals. `IncrementSheet` also keeps its `Theme.background` fill where other trays use `presentationBackground(Theme.surface)`; that is a fill decision, not a button, and changing it blind from Linux is a device-look change.

**Same round, third pass: the detent vocabulary — five sanctioned shapes, and `.fraction` retired as a place to invent numbers** — Dave: "audit which types of trays we're using across the app… Apple provides some options, like about how tall on initial open, whether expandable. Not saying we must settle on one treatment across the board, but that we should only vary for good reason."

Thirty-three detent declarations, and the variation is mostly principled: `[.medium, .large]` on 14 trays, `[.large]` on 5, `[.medium]` on 2, `[.height(n)]` on the 3 fixed-size pickers. Two findings.

**Four trays carried four different magic fractions — 0.84, 0.85, 0.88 and 0.70 — for the same job.** They are the exercise/session configuration family: the planning `ExerciseDetailSheet`, the mid-workout `SessionExerciseSheet`, the `SessionOverviewSheet` they open from, and `ExerciseConfigSheet`. Nobody can tell 0.84 from 0.85 on a device, none carried a comment, and the pattern is obviously copy-then-nudge. They share one named `PresentationDetent.appTall` (0.85) now, defined next to `SheetHeader` with the reason: a tray you open ON a routine or ON a live workout, which keeps a sliver of that context visible so it reads as a sheet rather than a screen you navigated to. `ExerciseConfigSheet` also gave up its `.large` second detent — at 0.85 there is essentially nothing left to expand into, and its three siblings don't offer it.

⚠️ **A `.height` detent must not be paired with `.medium`.** `IncrementSheet` declared `[.height(300), .medium]`. `.medium` is a FRACTION of the screen, so on a small phone (an SE at 667 pt, medium ≈ 333) it lands BELOW the fixed height — dragging the sheet "up" to its second detent makes it SHORTER. Fixed-size content gets one `.height` and nothing else, which also means the system stops drawing a grabber, matching the two other fixed pickers.

**The rest of the variation earned itself and stays**, now written down where the next tray will see it: `[.medium, .large]` is the default for anything you browse or edit; `[.large]` alone is a whole task that would be unusable at half (the equipment-resolve flows, the picker, the mascot demo); `[.medium]` alone is a short fixed tray that shouldn't resize (the Health primer, the Data tray); `SwapInSheet`'s three-detent `[scaledHeight, .medium, .large]` rides its two-stage slide, which is exactly the "good reason" the rule allows for.

**SETTLED, same day, by Dave looking at one of each: A TRAY IS A PAGE.** He was shown one tray of each idiom — the Kits switcher (A) and the routine exercise sheet (B) — and answered "the Kits sheet is dramatically better". So the law is: **a tray fills `Theme.background`, and its content sits ON it as `Theme.surface` cards.** All 20 sheet fills in the app are `Theme.background` now, with no exceptions — including the mascot demo, whose fill is a uniform plane either way and which was left `surface` only by inertia (an exception nobody can see is still an exception the next tray copies).

**It also lands on the better ground for contrast, which the colour round's own checker confirms.** That round (merged into this branch the same day) measures selection and advisory text over BOTH grounds, and `page` beats `surface` in every paired case, in both schemes: the selected label on its wash reads 6.66:1 over page against 6.02:1 over surface in light, 6.77 against 6.02 in dark; the selection ring 3.69 against 3.43, and 4.71 against 4.26. That direction is structural, not luck — `background` is the extreme end of the neutral scale in each scheme (white in light, the darkest charcoal in dark), so anything sitting on it has more room than it does on the middle rung. `scripts/contrast-check.py` reports 0 failures after the merge. ⚠️ Worth knowing for the reverse case: moving content ONTO `surface` is the direction that costs contrast, and that is the ground several of those measurements were originally written against.

⚠️ **Converting an idiom-B tray is TWO moves, not one.** Flipping only `presentationBackground` would leave its blocks filled `background` on a `background` sheet, visible as a border and nothing else. Every well went with its sheet: `ExerciseDetailSheet` (its metrics block + both `HeartRateTargetSheet` row fills, including the `isSelected ? selectedTint : …` pair), `SessionOverviewSheet` + `SessionExerciseSheet`, `ExerciseConfigSheet`, `ExerciseEditorView` (three text-field wells + the defaults block), and the six `RevealSurface` trays through their shared `.sheet(item:)`. ⚠️ `RevealSurface:95` stays `background` on purpose — that is the DRAWER BODY behind the sliding TabView, not a tray. The three trays with no blocks at all (`ExerciseSwapTray`, and the metric and rep pickers) just took the fill.

The finding this replaces, kept because it explains why the split existed and was never sloppiness:

⚠️ **The tray fill split 11 `Theme.surface` / 9 `Theme.background`, and the reason was TWO COMPETING ELEVATION IDIOMS, not carelessness.** `Theme` is a ladder — `background` (the page) → `surface` (cards, sheets, pills) → `surfaceRaised` (things on a card) — and the trays use it two opposite ways. **A: the tray is a PAGE** (fill `background`, content sits on it as `surface` cards) — `RoutineTemplateDetailScreen`, `AddToRoutineSheet`, `EquipmentResolveSheet`, `EquipmentLibraryTray`. **B: the tray is a CARD** (fill `surface`, rows recessed as `background` wells, a step back DOWN the ladder) — `ExerciseDetailSheet`, `SessionOverviewSheet`, `ExerciseConfigSheet`, `HeartRateTargetSheet`. Every tray is internally consistent: nobody put a `surface` card on a `surface` sheet, where it would vanish. So the nine `background` trays are not drifting from `surface`'s own "Cards, sheets, pills" doc comment — they are paying for the cards they contain. What the app never did is pick between the idioms. ⚠️ That also set the price, and is why this was flagged rather than guessed: it was never nine `presentationBackground` flips, it was "does a tray read as a page or as a card", and the losing idiom's INNER fills move too. The two rungs are ~6% apart in luminance (`#2A2925` vs `#201F1D` in dark), which is exactly the kind of call a screenshot settles and Linux can't (the `.background(.bar)` precedent, 2026-07-26) — so it went to Dave as an A/B, and came back in one word.

⚠️ **Device pass, and it is the whole round** — scroll feel is CI-invisible. Open the exercise editor's DEFAULTS tray and scrub every row: weight (does half-a-pound grain feel twitchy at 3 pt/unit, and is 0→225 lb a reasonable flick?), reps, height, pace on both an erg exercise and a run, speed, incline, power, cadence. Then the rep tray: does parking "up to" on the target read as "no range", and does the composed readout ("8–12") make that obvious? Then the live set screen, where the weight and reps cards now open tapes mid-workout — the one place the pick is under time pressure. On the header round: the metric tray and the rep tray at their new detents (320 / 430) — a `SheetHeader` is taller than the nav bar it replaced, so the check is that nothing crowds or leaves a dead band — the Increment tray's new title row, and the four hand-rolled tray headers clearing the grabber at `.medium`. Kit 512 + CLI 26 green on Linux.
**2026-07-28 (later) — The colour audit round: one selection look, kit names read as names, and the widgets stop ignoring Increase Contrast** — a sweep of every colour call site against the documented grammar (green data · blue selection · purple done · amber advisory · ink/cream actions), then Dave item by item. The system held nearly everywhere — twelve system-colour uses app-wide, no hex outside the palettes — so this is the short list that didn't.

**Selection is a TINTED GROUND, a ring, and bright text — never a solid fill** (Dave: "I like the green design better, like with the stroke, dark background, and bright text all in shades of green. Do that, but blue, and apply around the app"). The increment sheet had been selecting in GREEN, which was the finding; the treatment it used turned out to be the one worth keeping. This is less a new look than the end of a half-finished conversion: the exercise-detail rows and the resolve sheet's options already selected through `selectedTint`/`selectedRing`, so the app carried two selection looks and now carries one. `SelectableChip`, the increment chips and the schedule day circles all move; #210's solid fill is retired, and `Theme.onSelected` loses most of its callers with it. ⚠️ Device read: a solid fill is the louder signal at a glance, and seven small day circles is where that matters most — if a scheduled week reads weakly, the fix is a heavier wash on the circles (a second token), not a return to solid.

**A kit name inside a sentence wears the DATA-TAG treatment** (Dave: "it's unclear that 'main' in 'add barbell to main' is a kit — just reads like an incomplete sentence with 'main' as an adjective for a missing word"). New `KitTag` / `KitNamePhrase` (`Views/Components/KitNamePhrase.swift`): the name gets `CardTagCapsule`'s soft `surfaceRaised` r6 fill, sized from the surrounding text rather than pinned to caption2. ⚠️ **The bug only appears once a SECOND kit exists** — the naming law (2026-07-20) keeps prose on "your kit" until then — so the sentence stops parsing exactly when the app gets more complicated. ⚠️ **The name must come LAST.** SwiftUI cannot put a padded, rounded tag inside a wrapping `Text` run (an inline background takes no corner radius, and a view can't be interpolated into a run), so `KitNamePhrase` is an `HStack` on the first text baseline. Every string carrying a name today already ends on it. **Where a tag can't go, the name gets its NOUN instead**: mid-sentence prose uses the new `EquipmentLibrary.activeKitPhrase` ("your kit" / "the main kit"), and so do the resolve sheet's FILLED primary buttons — a soft `surfaceRaised` tag on a saturated cap reads as a hole punched in it.

**`BrandPalette` (PlusPlusShared) is the single source for the five hues.** They were restated in raw hex in three files, agreeing by luck; one cost was already live, since the widget extension's private copy carried no high-contrast variants and so ignored Increase Contrast while the app honoured it. Widgets read the shared values now, which fixes that by construction — a widget can no longer get a hue right and its HC pair wrong, because it states neither. `WTheme` is DELETED (`greenLight` was dead anyway); the Dynamic Island's always-dark pieces take a named `accentOnDark` rather than an adaptive value. ⚠️ The NEUTRALS deliberately stay in `Theme`: widgets draw on the system's ground, not the app's paper, and sharing them would imply a canvas the extension doesn't have. ⚠️ `WatchTheme` also stays — watchOS keeps the system's black canvas and only ever renders the dark side, and that target doesn't compile PlusPlusShared at all. The `Color(hex:)`/`Color(light:dark:)` initializers moved with the palette, and `UIColor(hex:)` widened from private to internal because `Theme.selectedTint` needs it for a per-scheme ALPHA — the one thing the adaptive pair can't express.

**Four smaller calls, each a token.** ⚠️ **REMOVE is neutral, never the destructive red** — Dave asked how it actually differs from DELETE, and the answer is the argument: REMOVE drops a piece from the ACTIVE KIT (the equipment still exists, still sits in other kits, and the same swipe puts it back), while DELETE ends the object everywhere and only exists on customs. Sharing one red made the single cue that separates them say they were the same act; neutral matches UNFAV on the exercise row, which is the identical turn-my-curation-off gesture. The **rest-countdown-beeps toggle** goes blue, joining the four preference switches it sits beside; the **kit toggle keeps green as a written-down exception** (it adds a thing to your kit, which is creation, not preference). **Amber stops standing in for destructive** in the resolve sheet's remove option — advisory is never an alarm. The **rep wheel** drops the system's cool `.secondary` for `Theme.textSecondary`. **`Theme.committedFill`** is retired to `Theme.done` (two names for one purple is how a palette forks).

**Nothing left open** (Dave: "on the stuff you dropped, don't drop — just go with your recs"). Both held items went in, and both turned out to be carrying a contrast failure the eyeball round had missed.

**The resolve sheet's primary is ink/cream now**, with the raised-key press and `Theme.keyRadius`. It filled blue, green, or blue again depending on which branch you landed in — so the button's colour tracked the SITUATION rather than what pressing it does, on the only screen in the app whose committing button wasn't `primaryFill`. It was also flat at radius 13, the one committing button that didn't depress.

**Amber got the token pair, and the pair got an ink.** `notesWash`/`notesRing` replace six alphas across five files — and measuring them to pick ONE value is what exposed two failures that had been shipping in all six: ⚠️ every existing amber ring (0.40–0.60) measured UNDER the 3:1 component floor (1.90:1 at 0.45 in light), so the token is 0.75; and ⚠️ **amber text on its own 14% wash measured 4.13:1 in light** — the identical failure the blue selection had, one hue over — so `notesInk` joins `selectedInk` at ~4.9/5.7. **That is the argument for tokens restated as evidence:** five hand-built copies is five places nobody measured, and the value of naming it is that it gets measured once.

**The HIG/WCAG pass on the round found three failures, one of them mine.** Measuring every changed pairing (the script is kept at `scripts/contrast-check.py`) rather than eyeballing it:

⚠️ **The new selection treatment did NOT clear AA, and that is inherent to swapping a fill for a tint.** `Theme.selected` as a label on its own 12% wash measures **4.07:1** over `surface` in light mode and 4.43:1 in dark — under the 4.5 floor, and the chip labels are footnote/semibold, which is not large text. The solid fill it replaced measured 5.32 / 7.22. Lowering the wash alpha doesn't rescue it (4.32:1 even at 8%), so the fix is a dedicated ink: **`selectedInk`** (0x0E4FA3 / 0x8CC4FA, with HC pairs) lands at **6.0:1 on the worst ground**. `selectedRing` also went 0.7 → 0.8, since 0.7 measured 2.89:1 against `surface`, just under the 3:1 component floor — and the ring matters more now that it outlines a tint instead of edging a fill. `selected` gained HC variants for the same reason. **Rule: a hue proven on a solid fill has NOT been shown to read on a wash of itself.**

⚠️ **Swipe blocks carry a WHITE label the app doesn't own** — `.swipeActions` draws titles white whatever the tint — so their fills have to be chosen for white, and the adaptive text hues are the wrong source. White on the dark-scheme green measured **1.97:1** and on the dark red **3.70:1**, both pre-existing and both worse than the neutral this round introduced. New `swipeAdd`/`swipeDelete`/`swipeNeutral`, FIXED in both schemes, at 5.4 / 5.4 / 6.7. (The first cut of the REMOVE fix used `textFaint`, which measures 3.18:1 — a legibility regression hiding inside a legibility fix.)

⚠️ **The set bar states done/live/upcoming in HUE alone**, and purple against green is **1.25:1** in light mode — a luminance near-tie (WCAG 1.4.1). `BlockBar` honours **Differentiate Without Color** now: done stays solid, upcoming becomes a hollow outline (the overview sheet's pip treatment), live grows taller. Shape carries all three where colour can't.

Also pre-existing, fixed while here: **advisory amber darkened a half-step in light** (0x9A6700 → 0x8F5F00), because 4.39:1 on `surface` is where most amber copy actually sits — the same remedy, for the same reason, as the `textFaint` darkening in the 2026-07-13 audit.

⚠️ Device-only: the washed selection against a solid one at arm's length (day circles first), a kit tag inside a headline and inside a list row at large Dynamic Type, the widgets under Increase Contrast (the one thing here that could not have been checked before this change existed), and the set bar with Differentiate Without Color on.

**2026-07-28 — The set screen says "prev:", prints no delta, and its bar carries the state grammar** — Dave, from a device screenshot of the set screen: change "last" to "prev:", kill the delta, the colour change and the carry-forward line; and colour the top bar by status, with the up-next block pulsing during rest.

**The corner is a reference, not a scoreboard.** It read `last 35 · +10` in accent green. Three things went with the word: the live delta, the green, and the faint "new weight carries to your remaining sets" beneath the steppers. What's left is `prev: 35` in `textFaint`. **"prev" is not a new word — it is the ledger's** (2026-07-27, Today's pending card): that card already sets a `target` column beside a `prev` column and prints no delta anywhere, for the reason that a signed number read as a *value* on Dave's device. The set screen was the last surface still doing the arithmetic for you, in the one place where the current value is enormous and directly above it. Same reasoning, one surface later: two numbers, no sign. The anti-shame rule that made green conditional on `improvementDirection` becomes moot when nothing is scored, so `deltaAnnotation` is `previousAnnotation` and returns a plain String.

**The carry-forward line went because the behaviour doesn't need narrating.** Logging a set with an edited load still rewrites the remaining sets' targets — untouched, and still covered by the target assertions in `SessionNavigationTests`. `WorkoutSession.weightCarriesForward` existed solely to drive that sentence (its doc comment said as much), so it was deleted with it rather than left as an unreferenced method with tests standing guard over nothing.

**The set bar now speaks done/live/upcoming.** It was plain progress in accent green; it now wears the app's colours — landed sets `Theme.done` purple, the set you're on `Theme.accent` green, the rest inert — which is the grammar the mid-workout overview has carried since #421, arriving on the surface you actually spend the workout looking at. ⚠️ **Status is read PER LOG, never `index < filled`**: a jump/redo completes sets out of order, and a left-to-right fill would then paint the wrong blocks purple. `filled` survives only as the VoiceOver count. It also **widened from a 20 pt inset to the 16 pt content column** (Dave), which is Today's week bar exactly — and, more to the point, the same column as the metric cards and the Log-set dock directly beneath it, so the bar had been narrower than everything it shares a screen with.

**Which turned out to be three columns, not two, so the whole live-session surface is on 16 now** (Dave: "fix that slight column misalignment"). The screen was running the header keys at 14, the bar and cards and dock at 16, and the exercise title at 20 — every element on the set screen inset differently from the one above it, at differences (2 pt, 4 pt) too small to read as intent and too large to read as aligned. ⚠️ `RaisedKeyStyle` insets only the BOTTOM (by its travel), so a key's cap edge lands exactly on its container's padding — the End key really was overhanging the bar by 2 pt, not optically compensating for anything. The rule drawn: **everything that renders under the progress bar shares its column** — header, exercise header, `pausedView`, `RestView`. The finished screen is deliberately NOT included: the bar is hidden there, and it's a centred celebration composition that is internally consistent at 20 with nothing to disagree with. ⚠️ This widens `RestView`'s three-key row by 8 pt, a week after that row was tuned (build 154) — more room, so the fit can only improve, but it wants an eye on the device pass.

**During a rest the live block BREATHES**, and that lands right by accident of an existing invariant: the cursor advances at log time, so while the countdown runs the green block is already the set you are about to do. The bar is therefore showing the up-next set waiting for you, which is what Dave asked for, with no notion of "next" needed anywhere in the code. It stops on pause for free — pausing banks the rest and clears `restEndDate`, which is the same flag the overview's pulse rides. The breathe lives in `BlockBar` (a `states:`/`breathing:` mode; nil states keeps the plain progress bar the week strip uses) and the CALLER honours Reduce Motion, per WCAG 2.3.3 — same contract, same 0.85 s ease, as the overview's pulse.

**Same round, the one backwards swipe in the app: the rail's DUPE moved to the LEADING edge.** A sweep of every swipe site (five native `.swipeActions` in `CatalogScopeView`, one `SwipeRevealRow` on the routine rail) found the catalogs already correct — leading FAV / kit ADD-REMOVE, trailing DELETE — and exactly one violation: the rail put DUPE and DELETE in the SAME trailing block, so a duplicate lived one thumb-width from a delete, on the edge that means "destroy" everywhere else. It is now DUPE leading / DELETE trailing, one 58 pt button each. ⚠️ Two consequences, both device-only. `RoutineDetailView` had to become a `.leadingRevealHost(active:)` — a pushed screen's full-width back-swipe pan begins at ~10 pt while a reveal needs 16, so without it the pop wins every rightward drag and DUPE is simply unreachable; it is gated OFF while routine settings is pushed on top, so that screen keeps its full-width pop. And it puts a rightward drag on the row whose leading column carries the superset RING: safe in principle, because the ring is a long PRESS and `SwipeGestureLayer` judges translation before taking a touch, but this is the app's most gesture-loaded region and the commit is independently revertible.

⚠️ Device-only, all of it: the corner at large Dynamic Type, the card now ending on the stepper keys, purple-vs-green legibility in the bar at 9 pt in both schemes, the breathe starting and stopping with the countdown (including across a pause, and across a transition into a new exercise, where the bar re-scopes to the new block), and on the rail — DUPE revealing rightward, DELETE leftward, the ring drag still forming supersets, and back-swipe still popping from the screen edge.

**2026-07-28 — `alignmentGuide` on an overlay child does NOT reposition it; a zero-height base with a bottom-aligned overlay does** — the build-154 device shot: the pull's answer rendered ON the week tally instead of in the gap above it. The mechanism shipped the day before was `.overlay(alignment: .top)` on the scroll's content stack with `alignmentGuide(.top) { $0[.bottom] }` on the line, the documented way to say "put my bottom edge on your top edge". SwiftUI ignored it — the line drew at the content's top edge, colliding with the first row, which on Today is the sticky week band. ⚠️ **Don't hang an overlay's position on a custom alignment guide.** The construction that works uses nothing but stock alignment: a zero-height `Color.clear` as the content's first child, with the line as its `.overlay(alignment: .bottom)`. The base's bottom edge IS the content's top, so bottom-aligning the overlay to it puts the line entirely above, reserving nothing and clipped by the scroll view at rest — which is what makes it appear only in the space the pull opens, where the system spinner used to be. It needs `.fixedSize()`, since a zero-height base proposes zero height to its overlay. Everything else about the line is unchanged: the 1.1 s hold, "Syncing…" before the network, the tail-clear, the killed spinner.

**2026-07-27 (later) — Pause is available MID-REST, because the rest now survives a pause; and the paused screen says where the pause caught you** — Dave: "I noticed we hide the workout's pause button during rest. Do we need to? If not, don't."

**We did need to, and the reason is a bug rather than a rule, so it got fixed instead.** `restEndDate` is a WALL-CLOCK instant. The paused screen replaces `RestView` entirely, so while paused nothing ticks — but the date keeps arriving, and on resume the remounting view computes zero remaining and `onAppear` fires `onEnd()`. Pause mid-rest and the rest is silently eaten. Hiding the key was the cheap answer; banking the interval and re-basing it on resume is the real one, and it's the same trick the interval timer already uses (`pausedRemaining`). A rest is also exactly when something interrupts you, so it's the moment the key is most wanted.

**⚠️ The banking happens in the Pause and Resume KEYS, not in `.onChange(of: session.isPaused)`.** An `onChange` fires AFTER the render that observed the flip, so the first paused frame would read un-banked state — and worse, on the way back `RestView` would remount against the still-expired date and could fire `onEnd()` before the re-base landed, which is a race for the exact bug this exists to prevent. Both functions are guarded on their own state, so neither can double-apply, and the two keys are the only paths that pause or resume a running clock (`engageClockIfNeeded` is gated on `!isWorkoutStarted`). The island drops to the WORKING phase while a rest is held, since a countdown that keeps running to zero behind a paused workout is worse than no countdown. (The activity's ELAPSED timer still advances through any pause — pre-existing, untouched here.)

**The paused screen gained ON HOLD and UP NEXT**, in the rest screen's card anatomy. It used to show a frozen clock and nothing else, so coming back to the phone told you the workout was held but not what from. Mid-rest that's the sharpest gap, because the screen you'd otherwise be looking at is the one naming the set you're about to do. ON HOLD reads "Rest · 0:42 left" (the BANKED interval, since the countdown is stopped and a live clock there would be a lie) or "Bench Press · set 3"; UP NEXT is the set the rest leads into, or, mid-exercise, the next DIFFERENT exercise — the next set of what you're already on is the same answer as ON HOLD, and repeating it says nothing. UP NEXT is omitted rather than emptied when the pause caught the last set.

**⚠️ A banked rest has TWO representations, and everything that cancels or creates a rest has to know about both** (swift-reviewer found two doors the first cut left open, both the same bug wearing different clothes). (a) `endRest()` guarded on `restEndDate != nil`, which is nil for the whole pause — and the overview's jump affordances reach it FROM the paused screen, so "skip to this exercise" returned early, left the bank intact, and Resume restored a countdown in front of the exercise you had just said to go to now (with the OLD rest's `restIsTransition` and denominator, and re-announced to the island and the wrist). It now cancels both. (b) The `LiveMirror.restChanged` handler is the one other path that sets a rest, and it wrote a wall-clock date unconditionally: a set logged on the WRIST while the phone sat paused parked a date behind the paused screen, and Resume ate it — the original bug through a door `bankRestForPause` doesn't own. It banks when `session.isPaused`.

**Banking also has a defensive `.onChange(of: isPaused)` on the PAUSE EDGE ONLY.** The bank-before-flip rule is otherwise convention held up by there being exactly two affordances, and #157 (Live Activity controls) would add a third that posts a notification. Safe on that edge because by the time `onChange` fires `isPaused` is already true, so the body has rendered `pausedView` and `RestView` is gone — no frame can observe the un-banked state. The RESUME side must stay in the button, where the ordering is the entire point.

**Not fixed, known, and worth naming.** (1) The Live Activity has no paused phase (`ContentState.Phase` is `.working`/`.resting`), so its elapsed count-up runs through any pause: pocket the phone paused and the island reads a workout that's still going. Pre-existing, but this change makes the paused state reachable at exactly the moment the phone goes away, so it stops being theoretical. (2) `restPausedRemaining` is view state while `isPaused` is persisted, so an app kill mid-paused-rest loses the rest silently — the same ephemerality `restEndDate` always had, now reachable across a relaunch. (3) The wrist is told nothing on a bank rather than `restEnded`: the op vocabulary has no "held" kind, and "the rest is over" is further from the truth than a rest it can't tick. Reviewer also flagged the resumed `restStarted(endsAt: now+70, total: 90)` as breaking the op's contract; it doesn't. A consumer infers the start as `endsAt - total`, which lands exactly 20 s back, which is exactly how far into the rest you are.

⚠️ Device-only: pausing mid-rest and resuming to confirm the countdown picks up where it left off (and the blocks with it), a jump from the overview WHILE paused mid-rest (no phantom countdown on Resume), the Lock Screen through a paused rest, and the card at large Dynamic Type.

**2026-07-27 — Rest is a dial, not a take-it-or-leave-it, and the rest screen has NO primary key** — Dave: make it possible to increase AND decrease rest mid workout, at a 15 second step (it was `+30s` or nothing). Three design rounds in one artifact settled the shape, and the second round was the one that mattered.

**The diagnosis Dave made, which is the durable part: Skip read as the thing to do.** It was the only `primaryFill` key on the screen, the widest, and it sat under the thumb. Three signals all pointing at ending recovery early, on a screen whose entire job is to finish by itself. The law that came out of it: **a screen that completes on its own has no primary act**, so nothing on the rest screen is filled except the two keys that ADJUST. Skip is a quiet cap. The recharge blocks are the one element that argues for waiting, and they gain weight simply by having no filled button to compete with.

**The stack is STATE · CONTROL · WHAT YOU'RE WAITING FOR** (Dave's ordering, with heart rate moved to the top on his last note): kicker, live vitals, countdown, recharge blocks, the three keys, and the UP NEXT card LAST. Two things fall out of putting the controls under the bars rather than at the bottom. The stepper sits directly beneath the thing it moves, so `+15s` and the blocks growing read as one gesture; and the controls now come BEFORE the card in the scroll, so at large accessibility sizes they stay above the fold instead of being pushed under it (the ScrollView exists for exactly that reason and previously worked against it). Up next loses nothing by going last, because it isn't tappable — it's what you read while loading the bar with the phone down at your side, which is where the bottom of the screen is easiest to see.

**The row is `Skip rest · −15s · +15s`, in that order, with the pair filled and Skip carrying an extra 6 pt of trailing gap.** The gap is load-bearing: `−15s` immediately beside Skip means a thumb stepping down twice would otherwise end the rest. All three keys use the SAME 4 pt travel rather than the 3 pt `.quietKey`, so their caps sit on one baseline — the quiet reading comes from the cap's fill, ink and type, not from a shorter key that would leave the row misaligned by a point. Skip is at its content width; the pair splits what's left. ⚠️ Flagged for the device pass: `primaryFill` is near-white in dark mode, so two of them under the green blocks may out-shout the countdown. The fallback is `Theme.surfaceRaised` + an ink border, which stays plainly dominant without taking the brightest-thing slot from the clock. One token either way.

**Subtracting stops one step short of zero, and that has to be enforced on the RESULT.** Skip is the way to zero, and a minus that could reach it would be a second way to do the same thing under a worse name. ⚠️ The first cut guarded the STARTING value ("more than one step left"), which still allows 16 s − 15 s: the countdown lands inside a second of zero, `onEnd()` fires, and the minus key has performed a Skip — from a one-second window that occurs in every single rest (swift-reviewer). `adjustRest(by:)` CLAMPS instead of refusing: `min(current, max(proposed, floor))`, where the outer `min` stops a clamp from ever running the rest UP when the clock is already inside the floor. Clamping is also what keeps the ISLAND honest, since a Live Activity button has no disabled state — near the floor its minus moves the clock to the floor rather than silently doing nothing. `restTotalSeconds` then moves by what was APPLIED, not by what was asked for, or a clamped subtract desyncs the blocks from the clock. On screen the key dims IN PLACE (never removed) so the row can't reflow mid-rest; on a 15 s SWITCH it starts dim, which is honest. ⚠️ Its enabled state reads `endDate.timeIntervalSinceNow`, NOT the tick's `remaining`: a `.periodic` `TimelineView` hands back the last SCHEDULED tick, so a re-render driven by anything else (a heart-rate sample, the other key) judges against a stale second, and a live key over a model that won't move is the build-76 silent-dead-tap class.

**The three-key row REFLOWS at accessibility sizes** to the pair over a full-width Skip (#164's reflow-don't-cap, as `DiffLedger` does). Three keys with a content-width label among them cannot hold one line at AX5 — "Skip rest" alone wants more than the whole content column there — and the two-key row this replaced could, because its fixed 5:7 fractions fitted. A regression, not an inherited risk. Stacking also states the escape/pair separation more plainly than a 6 pt inset can at that scale.

**⚠️ `restTotalSeconds` moves by the same delta as the end date.** It is the recharge blocks' denominator, and the stepper now sits directly under those blocks: left alone, `+15s` pushes `remaining` past the total and the bar just pins full, so the control appears to do nothing. Shifting both keeps the bar meaning "this rest, as it now stands" and makes it grow and shrink with the taps above it. This mattered less when the control was at the bottom of the screen; adjacency is what makes it a bug.

**The countdown beeps needed no change** — `onChange(of: remaining)` already guards the last-three-second ticks on a DECREMENT, so `+15s` can't fire them, and a `−15s` that lands inside the last three seconds beeps, which is correct.

**The island and Lock Screen carry the same row** (`RestControlButtons`): `RestAdjustment` is now `.add`/`.subtract`/`.skip` with a shared `stepSeconds`, plus a new `ReduceRestTimeIntent`, so the two surfaces can't disagree about the step. Both uses are full width (Lock Screen and the EXPANDED island's bottom region), so three keys fit. The watch rest screen stays Skip-only for now; `LiveMirror` already reflects a phone-side adjustment there.

**Heart rate is gated on this session having EVER had a reading, not on one being fresh**, and the row holds a minimum height once it's in. Two hazards, one shape. An ungated empty `HStack` in a `VStack(spacing: 20)` still holds its spacing open, which was invisible mid-screen and is a 40 pt hole directly above the clock now that it rides under the kicker (and the phone has no HR sensor, #418, so that's the common case). But gating on FRESHNESS — the predicate the labels themselves use, a 180 s window — would let the row come and go as readings age out, and since this stack is vertically centred, that shifts the countdown AND the three keys under a travelling thumb. Appearing once, at the first sample, is the only movement worth having.

**Known and deliberately not fixed here: the WATCH doesn't follow a phone-side adjustment.** `WatchLiveSession` folds the phone's `restStarted` op into its reducer, but `WorkoutRunView` drives its countdown from its own `@State restEndsAt` and schedules `WatchRestNotifier` at the original end date, so a phone `±15s` moves neither the wrist's clock nor its rest-over haptic. That's the deferred item from #322, not something this round broke — but this round promotes adjusting from a rare `+30s` hedge to the screen's two loudest keys, and the wrist haptic is the documented rest-over cue, so it is now worth fixing. Rides with whatever answer the watch gets on the stepper itself.

⚠️ Device-only, none of it CI-visible: the two filled caps' brightness against the clock in dark mode, the three-key row at large Dynamic Type (both the reflow and the sizes just under it), the dimmed minus on a short transition, the blocks growing and shrinking with the taps, and the island's three buttons on a real Lock Screen.

**2026-07-27 (last of the day) — Today's week strip is a STICKY band inside the scroll, the refresh line renders at the TOP of the scroll, and pull-to-refresh no longer re-anchors** — Dave's build-152 device report, two symptoms with one shape: "the 'x of n workouts this week' bar doesn't pull down with the rest of the Today view UI when I pull down after scrolling to the top. Also I don't see the little whimsy pieces of text that we cycle through for that interaction."

**The strip.** It was PINNED between the navigation bar and the scroll (one build old, 2026-07-26). What pinning missed is that **a large title travels**: on overscroll UIKit walks the large title DOWN with the rubber-banding content, while anything laid out OUTSIDE the scroll keeps the frame it was given — so "Today" slid down over the block bar, which is the screenshot. ⚠️ **The general law: anything a large title can travel over has to be scroll content.** A `safeAreaInset(edge: .top)` is pinned the same way and fails identically.

Plain scroll content isn't the answer on its own, though, and it took two cuts to land. It can't ride ABOVE the today anchor (the opening scroll seats today at the very top, so anything above the anchor is off-screen the moment you arrive, and the week tally is not something to scroll UP for), and below the anchor it scrolls away — which the first cut shipped and Dave rejected: he wanted **exactly main's behaviour, plus travel**. **So it is STICKY**: the strip is the scroll's first content, and a `visualEffect` climbs it back to the visible top while you scroll (`offset(y: minY < 0 ? -minY : 0)` against the `.scrollView` coordinate space) but does nothing on overscroll, where `minY` goes positive and the band simply rides the content down. Pinned at every scroll position, travelling on the pull, one formula. ⚠️ That `visualEffect` is a pure RENDER-TIME read: it writes no state, so it is not the layout-feeds-back-into-state pattern that breaks the search-role morph — and it is the only legal way to read scroll geometry here, since `onScrollGeometryChange` is exactly that banned pattern.

⚠️ **A sticky band FLOATS, so it stops reserving its own space** — and the opening `scrollTo(todayAnchorID, .top)` would then seat today's date line underneath it. The fix needs no measuring and no constant: **a hidden second copy of the band sits immediately below the anchor** (`weekStripBand.hidden()`), reserving exactly the right height at every Dynamic Type size and however the tally wraps. The band covers it on arrival; scrolled up to the week ahead it reads as the space the strip lives in. Two consequences that follow from floating: the band needs an OPAQUE background (rows slide under it — the same call the pinned search headings make), and the 16 pt content column moved from the scroll's content stack onto its children, because that background has to span the full width or rows show through the gutters.

**⚠️ And it does NOT ride the rail — Dave, reversing the first cut of this round.** That version gave it a spine and no node (like `beyondThisWeekBlock` and `todayMarker`) on the reasoning that the rail is continuous through every section of this timeline. Dave: "nah I don't want it to be inside the rail region." So the strip keeps the SCREEN's 16 pt content column and its full-width bar, and reads as a BAND across the surface — the week header it is — rather than a timeline entry indented into the caption column. Now that it is sticky the rail question mostly evaporates: the band lives above the whole timeline, and the only place a spine is missing is the hidden reserve at the seam between the week ahead and today.

**Build-153 device round, same day: the answer moved into the gap, and the spinner died.** Dave's screenshot showed the band pinning perfectly (so `.scrollView`'s origin IS the safe-area-adjusted visible top — the #1 unknown, settled) with "Keep stacking." rendered as page CONTENT below it, shoving the timeline down. His call: "should appear in the area revealed during the pull-down instead. Also, kill the loading spinner in there." So the line is an `overlay(alignment: .top)` whose `alignmentGuide(.top) { $0[.bottom] }` seats its BOTTOM on the content's top — entirely above the first row, reserving nothing, clipped by the scroll view at rest, and revealed by exactly the gesture that asks for it. Three consequences, all of them the same fact from different sides: **the answer is only visible while the gap is open**, and the system holds the gap open until the `refreshable` closure returns. (a) The closure now waits 1.1 s after setting the line, or a pull with nothing to fetch would flash it for a frame. (b) A connected sync says "Syncing…" BEFORE the network rather than after, since with the spinner gone an empty gap for the length of a network round-trip reads as a dead pull. (c) Clearing moved off a fixed timer started when the message is set — a sync can outlast any window, and an expired line would leave the open gap empty — onto the closure's tail, delayed by the snap-back so the line goes while it can't be watched going. ⚠️ **The spinner has no hide API**, so it is drawn in a CLEAR TINT (`.tint(.clear)` on the ScrollView) with `.tint(Theme.textPrimary)` restoring the app's ambient tint one level in, on the content. Today's is the app's only `.refreshable`, so nothing else inherits it.

**The quips.** Not a copy bug, a POSITION bug. The pull-to-refresh line rendered as the first child of the below-anchor lazy region, which is "inline where the pull settled" only on a timeline with nothing above the anchor. Dave has a mon/wed/fri schedule, so his week-ahead section is a screenful tall, and pull-to-refresh can only fire at the scroll view's actual TOP — a screenful above where the line inserted. It was landing off-screen every time. The line moves to the very top of the scroll content, which is the only place the gesture can start from.

**And the refresh stopped re-anchoring the scroll.** `dayToken += 1` (the refresh's honest re-derive of due-ness) also drove a `scrollTo(todayAnchorID, anchor: .top)`, on the reasoning that "the gesture asks for a fresh Today, and Today opens at today." That reasoning is reversed: a refresh must leave the surface where the pull left it. The jump yanks the view out from under the gesture, and it carried the pull's own answer off-screen — racing the refresh control's snap-back for whether the answer was visible at all. Re-anchoring is now `dayChangeToken`'s, bumped only by `NSCalendarDayChanged`, where the day genuinely moved and "today" is somewhere else.

**Same round: a sync that moved nothing gets a quip too.** `GitHubSyncCoordinator.summarize` returned "Up to date" for a pass with no pulls and no pushes; it returns nil now, and the refresh line falls back to `RefreshQuip.random()`. The quip already existed for the disconnected case on the reasoning that a dead pull deserves a friendly line instead of nothing — a connected pass with no news is the same dead pull, and "Up to date" is a non-answer in a voice that doesn't announce non-events. Real outcomes still report ("Updated from GitHub", "Backed up", "Couldn't sync. Try again."), and sync state has its own surfaces (the drawer's GitHub row + Sync now). ⚠️ Reversible in one line if Dave wants the confirmation back.

⚠️ App-target only, and every part of it is CI-invisible (overscroll rubber-band, a UIKit title's travel, a 3-second inline line): device pass = scroll to the top of Today, pull, and watch the strip and the title move together; confirm a quip appears at the top where you let go and clears itself; confirm the view does NOT jump to today after the pull; confirm the strip holds the top at every scroll position and that today's date line is NOT under it on arrival. ⚠️ **#1 check is where the band pins**: the whole design rests on `.scrollView`'s origin being the safe-area-adjusted visible top. If it turns out to be the scroll view's raw frame top, the band will pin up behind the navigation bar and the hidden reserve will read as a blank gap — the remedy is to offset the pin by the top safe area, not to abandon sticky. Second check is a tap in the band's height: if `visualEffect`'s offset does not carry the hit region, a tap there may reach the row underneath it.

**2026-07-27 (later) — The reveal drawer's swipe-to-open strip is `.simultaneousGesture`, and its direction verdict LATCHES for the whole touch sequence** — a real dead zone, found while investigating Dave's intermittent-scroll report, but ⚠️ **NOT the cause of that report** (see the entry below it). `RevealContainer` wraps the entire `TabView`, and its 16 pt swipe-to-open strip is the TOPMOST layer of that ZStack, running from y=104 to the bottom of the screen on every tab root (`canSwipeOpen` defaults TRUE, so it covers all five). That column sits exactly over the 16 pt leading margin of every list row. It was attached with `.gesture`, the modifier this repo's own #99 law says starves `UIScrollView`'s pan. The direction guard lived in `onChanged` and merely `return`ed on a vertical drag, so the drawer correctly did NOTHING — but the recognizer had already CLAIMED the sequence, and a claim is what decides whether the list scrolls. Net result: a flick that starts in the leftmost 16 pt is completely dead. ⚠️ **The law: guarding what a gesture DOES is not guarding what it CLAIMS.** #169's own text names that distinction ("changed threshold *decisions* but not gesture *claiming*") and pins it on `SwipeRevealRow`, which has used the correct `.simultaneousGesture` since #99. Fix: `.simultaneousGesture`, plus a `@GestureState` `openIntent` that latches the rightward-and-horizontal-dominant verdict on the sequence's FIRST event instead of re-asking per event — the old guard re-asked until one passed, so a scroll curving rightward partway down could still hand the drawer the touch mid-flick. A rejected sequence now stays rejected for its whole life, which is what lets the strip coexist with the scroll pan instead of racing it. ⚠️ CI-invisible by construction: XCUITest dispatches through accessibility and bypasses gesture overlays entirely (the same blind spot that hid the build-36 offset bug), so this needs a device pass — scroll each tab root with the thumb hard against the left edge, then confirm a deliberate rightward edge drag still opens the drawer and a diagonal one no longer hijacks a scroll. ⚠️ Do NOT read this entry as closing #169: the strip POSTDATES it (filed build 17; the drawer landed build 63), it is 16 pt wide, and Dave's reported symptom is mid-screen.

**2026-07-27 (last) — #169 ROOT-CAUSED: a SwiftUI `DragGesture` cannot decline a touch. Catalog rows go NATIVE `.swipeActions`; the rail's `SwipeRevealRow` goes to a UIKit directional pan** — Dave closed the hunt himself: "I just confirmed it has to do with the angle of my flick. It happens when I do too much horizontal motion." That is `SwipeRevealRow`, suspect #1 on #169, exactly as the issue was written three months earlier — and the two theories that came first (the reveal drawer's edge strip, a main-thread stall in the sync dirty gate) were both wrong, each killed by one fact: the symptom is mid-screen, and it happens without a tab switch. ⚠️ **The law, now stated twice in one day and worth learning once: guarding what a gesture DOES is not guarding what it CLAIMS.** Every direction check in that component lived inside `.updating`/`.onChanged`, so the guards decided what the row did; nothing decided what the recognizer took. At 16 pt with any horizontal component the drag engaged and the enclosing scroll pan was starved for the whole touch — the row refusing to move (guard says vertical) and the list refusing to move (the drag owns it) are the SAME event. `.simultaneousGesture` cannot fix this and never could: it governs SwiftUI's composition, not the claim. Checked against Axiom's iOS 26 SwiftUI gesture reference — there is no first-class API for directional arbitration; every remedy it offers puts the test inside the closure, which is the broken shape. **Fix, two halves, Dave's call ("both"):** (a) the three `CatalogScopeView` rows are in a real `List`, so they move to native `.swipeActions` — UIKit owns the arbitration, the swipe law (LEADING curation / TRAILING destructive) is unchanged, `allowsFullSwipe` lands #168 for free, and `openSwipeRow` plus the hand-mirrored `.accessibilityActions` are deleted (native publishes its own rotor actions; re-declaring reads every act twice). Accepted costs: the action blocks lose the mono-uppercase-on-solid look, and rotor labels become FAV/DELETE rather than sentence-case. (b) the routine-detail rail is in a `ScrollView`, so it keeps `SwipeRevealRow`, whose drag and tap are now `SwipeGestureLayer` — a `UIPanGestureRecognizer` declining vertical touches in `gestureRecognizerShouldBegin` (BEFORE the sequence is taken, which is the one thing SwiftUI cannot do) plus a `UITapGestureRecognizer` with `require(toFail:)` on it.
